ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-11_U663	The next item is the first reading of the draft law on reform of the local government organisation initiated by the Group of the Isamaa and Res Publica Union. Siim Kiisler, Member of the Riigikogu, will make a presentation on behalf of the initiators.
ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-11_U664	Honourable Chairman! Honourable Members of the Riigikogu! I will start with what Chancellor of Justice Indrek Teder emphasised five years ago, in 2009. I quote: 'The procedures of the Chancellor of Justice have shown that a very large part of the local government units in Estonia are too small to effectively and effectively exercise constitutional self-government. [---] I am of the opinion that a large number of municipalities are unable to guarantee the fundamental rights of individuals, the quality of their organisation and procedures according to the Constitution, as well as legislation, is a sufficient and compelling reason for the rapid and decisive intervention of the state and for the introduction of a new administrative arrangement. Estonia must become strong and functioning municipalities that ensure constitutional treatment for their residents, regulation of quality local governments and access to public services evenly across the country. The state authority has a constitutional obligation to establish administrative arrangements in which fundamental rights of persons would have been protected and the Constitution respected. The overall increase in the level of protection of fundamental rights potentially achieved by the decisive administrative reform should outweigh any other consequences for individuals and political parties, whether the loss of some officials' jobs or the loss of the party's political stronghold on the ground. Whether the state power can carry out a decisive administrative reform is actually an indication of the extent to which the state power is able to ensure compliance with constitutional obligations across Estonia. ' The proposed draft law aims to create a viable and comprehensive local government structure following the election of local government councils in October 2017. The proposed reform will result in an increase in the overall capacity of local authorities, thereby ensuring that citizens are treated more equally, regardless of their place of residence. The greater capacity of local authorities is ensured, in particular, by their sufficient size and overlap with the area of everyday life of their inhabitants. Size shall be measured in particular by the number of inhabitants. For example, the municipality of Märjamaa is five and a half times larger than Tallinn, yet in the context of the size of the municipalities, we are talking about Tallinn as much larger. Some facts as a reminder. The difference between the largest and the smallest local government in Estonia is 4133 times, while the difference between the budgets of these local governments is 5,265 times. The 40th rural municipality of Estonia and the city have fewer than 1000 inhabitants. Forty municipalities and cities, and the number of such municipalities is increasing! With fewer than 2000 inhabitants, half of Estonia's municipalities are located in Estonia. With less than 3,000 inhabitants, there are two thirds of our municipalities and cities. It is precisely where the geographical location is less favourable that our municipalities are generally smaller and more fragmented. These two factors, in combination, mean less capacity for the municipalities of such regions, and this also entails unequal treatment of the citizens of the municipalities to which the Chancellor of Justice has referred. The impact assessment carried out during the drafting of the draft has identified a number of areas where the impact can be considered significant. For further impact assessment, expert assessments were commissioned and possible impacts were discussed with industry ministries and other interested parties. Worked on a number of sectoral working groups. The explanatory memorandum to the draft proposal describes the effects very thoroughly, but I will briefly mention some of the more important. First of all, the population. Our population is shrinking and ageing. According to the baseline scenario drawn up by Statistics Estonia, the number of inhabitants in remote municipalities will decrease more rapidly by 2020, almost twice as fast as local authorities at marginalisation risk. Decreases in population, ageing and relocation have a direct impact on the sustainability of municipalities. As a result of the reform, the gender and age composition of the municipal population will be balanced. The reform will make up for particularly extreme situations where, for example, the majority of the population is common for older people, so the municipality's income base depends predominantly on state subsidies. The study concerning the merger of municipalities between 1996 and 1999 showed that the merger of the centre of attraction and the hinterland resulted in a logical whole. The merger eliminated the artificial inconveniences caused by the administrative border, as the inhabitants of the merging municipalities used common infrastructures, work and ownership relations were intertwined, services were provided in common buildings, etc. The new municipality had a higher number of inhabitants, a more stable age structure and better conditions for implementing sectoral policies. Second of all, the officer's office. In larger municipalities, officials can specialise in one area and use human resources more efficiently than they do now, hire officials full-time and pay them competitive wages. Such a municipality is an attractive employer for real professionals in its field. The most important and immediate shift in mergers is the cardinal change in the profile of the official body. In small municipalities there are 6 to 8 full-time officials, of whom, apart from the municipality's elder, accountant and parish secretary, are usually only social workers with a clear profile, rarely a few other key-time officials. In large municipalities, the number of officials who have focused on one particular task is increasing, so officials become more professional. According to the 2008 survey, the number of monofunctional officials in pre-accession municipalities studied increased from an average of 8 to 26. For example, in the large municipalities of Raplamaa there are about 90% of the monofunctional officials, but small municipalities need professional support from other municipalities in almost all positions. Financial opportunities. The investment capacity of the merged municipalities has increased several times, while the loan burden has remained the same. Investments per capita have been consistently higher in the merged municipalities than in the unjoined municipalities. One aspect of which leads the merged municipalities to achieve greater investment capacity is the increase in scale and the resulting greater fiscal investment capacity. Another important variable is the increase in loan capacity and the ability to use the loan to invest and develop the municipality. There is a clear link between the small size of the municipality and the increase in the share of government expenditure: the merged municipalities do not aim for savings in government expenditure, but rather for increased expertise and adequate remuneration, which led to an increase in government expenditure in all of them, but a decrease in the share of government expenditure in the budget. The overview of municipal social benefits shows that the financial volume and qualification of the benefits vary widely. In the case of accession, social protection for the inhabitants of the region can be guaranteed on a uniform basis. If the capacity of the municipality is greater, those regions where more social problems have accumulated can be helped to create better living conditions for more deprived and needy members of society whose chances are lower than those of the rest. Education. In small municipalities, according to the survey, two to three times more budget money was spent per student than in large municipalities. This was both due to higher wage costs for teachers and to higher management costs. During the 2006-2011 period, educational costs per pupil increased by 40% in small municipalities, while the merged municipalities increased by 10%. Thus, in small municipalities, the necessary level of education can only be guaranteed at the expense of other sectors. According to experts who studied foreign traineeships, it is incomprehensible how the municipality can maintain the right to organise education and the possibility, for example, where 75% of children do not go to schools in the same municipality. The business environment. If the administrative boundaries of local government overlap with the actual area of activity of people and the jobs are located close to home, the daily movement of the oppressive majority of people will remain within the borders of one municipality. Today, over 70% of people living outside the city move to work outside their municipality of residence. The reform will create real motivation for municipalities to engage in business development, as most of the rural municipality's people live and work in the same field. As entrepreneurship develops, the income from personal income tax will also increase in the local government's budget. Creating business areas, activating people and preparing for the needs of the labour market, negotiating with businesses and using other entrepreneurship and labour market activation mechanisms are tasks that are clearly beyond the reach of small self-governments. Finally, civil society and local democracy. The remoteness of authorities and peripheralisation is one of the central fears in the planning of mergers. At the same time, interviews conducted in the course of the studies have pointed out that the periphery was already a periphery before accession, and this trend can only be slowed down. Merging the Centre and its logical hinterland will strengthen the cohesion of the city and the land, as administrative boundaries will disappear when the approach to development starts to be based on the principle of the integrity of the municipality. The broadening of the borders increases the strategic nature of the activities of the rural municipalities and gives them greater responsibility to shape local life in a larger picture than before. Qualitative analyses, as well as surveys, have confirmed that villages and a sense of community unity will be activated in the merged municipality. As a result of accession, citizens will become more aware of the importance of their organisation and active citizenship. Organised citizens have a greater influence on all municipal policies, not just on solving individual problems. In the polls, almost half of citizens and most officials found that community unity has increased since local governments joined. In the merged municipalities, competition for council seats is growing, and citizens will have more choice as to which candidates to support. This, in turn, extends local democracy and makes it possible to balance differences of opinion. According to a study carried out in 2012, 43% of household representatives had visited the local parish house or city government in the last 12 months. Thus, there is no reason to fear that officials will move away from the population. Meeting with officials is not so much important, but the quality of the meeting, whether the citizen can solve his or her problem or proposal. The latter depends on the official's expertise, which can certainly be guaranteed more in a larger municipality than in a small municipality, offering the official a challenging job and a higher salary. Honourable Riigikogu! In 1938 and 1939, a rural municipality reform based on Christaller's theory of centres was carried out under the leadership of the government, which left 248 rural municipalities in Estonia instead of 372. Now it's been 75 years. Life has changed a lot, and long ago it is time to take another strong step. Thank you.

The tasks are all the same, but as I said, if two thirds of the population are less than 3,000, then ultimately they select the tasks: they choose and say that there is no money for everything, we do some things, some things remain unfulfilled. And minority municipalities then really try to carry out all the tasks and perhaps also to assume voluntary tasks that are not directly attributable to the law. It follows from the fact that the smaller municipalities, which are in the majority, carry out the tasks assigned to them selectively, is what the Chancellor of Justice says: citizens are treated unequally. Another consequence is that our ministries, referring to the fact that municipalities cannot carry out their tasks by referring to these same mini-governments, often want to take on more things to organise a sub-office of the central government or some ministry. I believe that municipalities should be stronger. Some of the tasks currently performed by the state could rather be carried out by municipalities. But the prerequisite for this is a certain capacity. There is a great deal of talk about this in the Nordic countries, where it analyses what the limit should be, from which additional tasks can be assigned to municipalities. The Nordic experts say that the minimum size of the tasks that the local authorities have could be 30000 inhabitants. The political agreement in the countries is generally that there could be 20000 inhabitants.

This is one place where we have clearly once again lost our competitiveness in these countries. We have to be honest about this. As far as the capacity of local authorities is concerned, it is indeed, broadly speaking, made up of three important factors or is most affected by three important factors. The local government's geographical location, of course, is easier for it to be able to be able to if it is located close to an international airport, an international port, there are many good conditions for job creation, compared to some places in South-East Estonia, for example. This certainly has a very strong impact on the capacity of local authorities. Secondly, the capacity of municipalities is influenced by the number of inhabitants. All studies, if you look at the local authorities' capacity tables, which, during my appointment as minister, began to be drawn up and have been drawn up for many years in a row, changes there, then clearly affect the table on the size of the local government. Thirdly, the decisions of the local authorities will certainly also be affected. When stupid decisions are made, they are less capable, and when smarter decisions are made, they are more capable. But you can't jump over your shadow. That's also clear. As I have pointed out here, there is no education with a hundred inhabitants.

ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-11_U769	Thank you. The role of the state has become weak. The interesting thing about this whole thing is that all political parties in Parliament have supported, at least during the last two Riigikogu memberships, the proposal to pay extra money from the state budget in order for the municipalities to join. In other words, all political parties in Parliament feel that accession is necessary. If they didn't think so, it would be a very stupid decision to pay for such a thing. And this support has been increased several times, it has been growing all the time. I would point out, in comparison, that in Saxony, for example, the Federal State of Germany has twice as much per capita support as we do, but the minimum rate is lower than we do. We are already completely comparable in size to the rich countries that pay accession grants. Parliament is very kind, very happy to increase this support for accession. The same way they want to be joined, but the local leaders and other local leaders say that the role of the state, the guiding hand of the state is necessary, otherwise this thing will not move forward. That's what the local government leaders admit. This draft law proposes how the central government would take a leading role in this process. As far as the example of Kohtla-Järve and surrounding municipalities is concerned, one major problem is that, of course, residents of small rural municipalities want to consume the services provided in the city. They want to put children in a community school, as I pointed out earlier, to consume various kinds of infrastructure, but often they don't have the money to pay the city for it. A rather typical situation that has been repeatedly described to me is, for example, this. The resident of the municipality wishes to put his child into the music school in the city, but the city says that since you are not a citizen of our city, you either have to pay for it further or the municipality will pay for it. Vald says there's no money to pay for it. A man is trapped in there sometimes. ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-11_U786	Mr President of the sitting! Ladies and gentlemen, The draft law on reform of the local government organisation, which we are discussing today, deals with a topic that has been discussed in Estonia for a long time. The reform of the local government system, together with the reform of the state administration in general, is indispensable. Change is needed, society is ready for change, and any effort towards more efficient administration is worth considering carefully the results achieved. This is likely to result from the decision of the commission to leave the draft in Riigikogu proceedings. Certainly, the drafters have done a great deal of work and collected a large number of necessary data. The conclusions and solutions that have been reached, however, are not one that can be supported. Unfortunately, this is not a law on the reform of the municipal administration, although the title of the draft states so. We can read from the explanatory memorandum to the draft that it is only after the adoption of the draft that the initiators are planning to start analysing the general organisation of the local government. The draft cannot therefore itself be a draft law on the reform of the municipal administration. It is true that it is rather a draft law on the territorial integration of municipalities. After all, the draft explanatory memorandum has pointed out on several occasions that the functions and revenue base of the municipalities, the municipal services and the standards imposed on them, as well as possible financing models, will all be discussed after the adoption of the draft. It is also stated in the draft explanatory memorandum that the tasks of rural governments and ministries and their sub-offices will also be reviewed for the final implementation of the reform with a view to transferring tasks of a self-governing nature to local authorities where possible. Shouldn't the reform have started with these operations? It is unfortunate that the initiators of the bill have also succeeded in misleading the public. The same is often asked as my good colleague Peep Aru asked: will there be or will there not be an administrative reform? And by this, it means exactly the same as my colleague, Peep Argi, meant forced merger of local governments. Since the administrative reform is not merely a merger of municipalities, the drafters have succeeded in skilfully and over a long period of time. Let's put it together, and then let's look at it. It's been a strategy so far. Will there be an administrative reform? This question can only be answered in the way things are: administrative reform is inevitable. With regard to harmonisation of the level of services, guaranteeing the fundamental rights of citizens and cooperation between local authorities, the administrative reform certainly also includes a coercion. The forced merger of municipalities before discussing the actual content of the matter, as the draft proposal is, cannot be supported. There are very many places where I would like to express my opinion, but because of limited time, I will bring out three aspects of the draft. First of all, the fact that the draft ignores cooperation between municipalities. In any event, the regional cooperation of the municipalities is declared unsustainable. While the explanatory memorandum speaks in theory of possible sectoral cooperation, according to which in each area of cooperation a cooperation organisation would function in its own right, how would these various organisations with different boundaries and membership numbers gather, which principles would work, we cannot get a sense of that. How and with what structure there would be coordination between services, such as the interconnection between public transport and the school network, we did not know about that either. The fact that even if municipalities join the principle of centres of attraction, the need for strong and coordinated cooperation will in any case remain, does not take the draft into account. Rather, the draft refers to the possibility that the functions currently performed in cooperation between counties or through rural governments could be fragmented between municipalities. This essentially eliminates the county. Secondly, the draft states that standardisation of the services provided by the municipalities threatens the autonomy of the municipalities, but that their coercion does not. I recall that both the Chancellor of Justice and the OECD have drawn attention to the differences in the level of services in local governments, particularly as regards the guarantee of fundamental rights. How can a clearer description of a person's fundamental rights be considered to be an abuse of the autonomy of local governments, but not a coercion of local governments? In general local government theory, we have two paths to choose from: the municipality either as an extension of the state's arm or a municipality based on local, community organisation and the right to self-determination. If we were to continue in the direction proposed by the draft, we would soon arrive at the fact that there are four or fewer municipalities in Estonia. I'm sure it would be cost effective, but it wouldn't have anything to do with the local arrangements. Thirdly, according to the draft, the functions and funding of the municipalities would only be analysed after the merger of the municipalities. This approach does not make sense. The functions, funding, local democracy and independence of the local authorities from central power are the cornerstones on which the local government remains at all. How is it possible to determine the size of the municipality first and then its tasks and funding principles? It's more like the method that we cut first, then we read. The government coalition has begun to do the work that the draft authors considered to be irrelevant to the development of a model of administrative reform based on the functionality of local governments. What will happen next from this draft will be shown by time and procedure. Admittedly, a lot of work has been done and a lot of work is still to be done. As the draft currently stands, it certainly cannot be implemented. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-11_U792	President-in-Office of the Council. Ladies and gentlemen of the House! The reform of Estonian self-government, or rather, the administrative-territorial reform, has been the subject of a political debate since 1997, more seriously since 2000, i.e. now more than 14 years. Most of the time, the reform plans have receded to drawing letters of land maps. Planning substantive changes that would make people's lives better has less of a will. Once again, a mere line of borders is hidden behind the name of the reform of the centres of attraction. There hasn't been a lot of disputed places in the past, but they've been substantive for that. Does the merger have to be forced or voluntary? A voluntary merger shall be supported by the State in accordance with the law on the facilitation of the merger of the local municipality. Can we say that the municipality is too small and that it should join the others if, for example, it has fewer than 5 000 inhabitants? It is believed that the number of inhabitants and the capacity of local authorities are correlated. However, this has not always been confirmed. Estonia's largest municipality is struggling to maintain its streets and provide day-care facilities. Hence, the problem is not about size, but about local authority and its political preferences. ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-11_U814	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. In addition to Siim Valmar Kiisler, representative of the initiator of the draft, the Head of the Regional Administration Department of the Ministry of Interior, Väino Truthmets, was invited to the meeting. Siim Valmar Kisler gave an overview of the bill, which we have been listening to here for the last two hours. After that, the representative of the Ministry of the Interior Väino Truth Forests received the floor, who pointed out that the Government of the Republic discussed this draft on 15 May. a and decided not to support the draft on the basis of the opinion of the three ministries. He also noted that according to the Coalition Agreement, the Government of the Republic aims to develop a municipal reform plan by 2015 which would clearly define at what level the tasks to be carried out and how the necessary funding for the performance of these tasks would be carried out. Before going to the questions and answers, it was agreed that before the draft was sent to the first reading, representatives of the Association of Estonian Cities and the Association of Estonian Rural Governments would certainly meet. Most of the questions we had in the Commission have actually sounded in this Chamber today and received almost literally the same answers. In pointing out something that has not yet been said here today, one not so much related to this bill, but still the substantive question was about the greater accession that is taking place in Saaremaa. The question was that, if you use the opportunity to join in the inter-election period, it is possible to conduct interviews in the e-vote environment, but in this environment, it is not possible to take decisions, let's say, through an e-vote. This information was new and was allowed to be passed on to the Home Office. There were repeated questions as to why, before the question of borders and only then the substantive work, whether these borders were the most important. Finally, at this meeting, it was decided to consensually propose that the draft be placed on the plenary agenda on 10 June and that Kalev Lillo, a member of the Committee on Rural Affairs, be appointed rapporteur. For the second time, the Committee on Rural Affairs discussed the draft at its meeting on 3 June. Märt Moll, Adviser to the Association of Estonian Rural Governments, Tiit Kirss and Anne Läns were then involved in the discussion. Märt Moll noted that the draft was strictly aimed at merging municipalities by merging territories. Substantial issues such as the functions of the municipality, cooperation between municipalities and the income base have been left unresolved. There is a lack of clarity about the tasks to be undertaken and how local authorities will have to perform as a result of the reform. The Association of Estonian Rural Governments cannot accept that the greater capacity of local authorities is ensured primarily by an adequate size. The process of designating the extraction centres has been controversial and the results are questionable. It is unclear how the merger of local governments through the unification of territories will develop local democracy. The Estonian Association of Rural Governments does not support this draft. Anne Läns, the representative of the Estonian Association of Cities, added that the preparation of the draft has taken place for a long time, but unfortunately it has been a process like a monologue and no dialogue has arisen. The title of the draft does not correspond to the content. The title of the draft is 'Restructuring the organisation of own government', but the content is the reform of the administrative-territorial organisation. He noted that the Union of Estonian Cities likes the current coalition agenda. One of its objectives is to develop a municipal reform plan by 2015 which clearly defines which tasks will be carried out and how they will be financed. He confirmed that the Estonian Cities Association is in favour of administrative reform, is prepared to deal with it and cooperate in this regard, but cannot agree to the programme for the reform of the centres of attraction. In the round of questions and answers, we wanted to know on what basis the claim that the process of designating centres of attraction has been of dubious value. In response, it was said that a model of attraction centres was proposed, but the attraction centres were mentioned by the rural authorities and, to the knowledge of the rural municipalities' association, no agreement was reached in more than half of the counties that this was a vision presented by the rural elders, which did not match the vision of the municipalities. It was also asked that since there have been several reform programmes, which one has been the most sensible from the perspective of the municipalities. The Municipalities replied that they considered that the most sensible minister was Reimaa's plan. There was also the question of how much the opinion of local governments was taken into account in the drafting of the law. It was sad to hear the answer, according to which none of their fundamental views were taken into account. After the discussion, it was decided to propose that the first reading of the draft be closed. It was given 5 votes, with 3 abstentions. It was decided to offer ten working days by the deadline for the 647 amendments to the bill. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-11_U851	We will move on to the 16th item, which is the second reading of draft law amending the State Family Benefits Act and other laws initiated by the Government of the Republic. I ask for a presentation of the Chair of the Riigikogu, Chairman of the Social Affairs Committee Heljo Pikhof!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-11_U852	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Happy Members of the Riigikogu! The Committee on Social Affairs has trusted me to reflect what happened in the Commission. Introducing Bill 669. The first reading of the draft Act amending the State Family Benefits Act, the Social Welfare Act, and the Law on the Grant of International Protection to Aliens, which was initiated by the Government of the Republic on 12 May 2014, was completed on 21 May. On the proposal of the Social Committee, the President of the Riigikogu set the deadline for amendments at 11 a.m. on 2 June 2014. Amendments were tabled by the Group of the Isamaa and Res Publica Union and the Group of the Estonian Centre Party. Following the completion of the first reading of the draft, the Committee on Social Affairs met at two meetings on 3 and 9 June to discuss the draft and prepare the second reading. At its meeting on 3 June, when the draft text was discussed, the Committee on Social Affairs decided to propose one amendment. Riina Saar, Head Specialist of the Social Security Department and Kaili Hendla, Adviser to the Welfare Department, participated in the substantive discussion of the amendments at the meeting on 9 June. The amendments tabled were explained and the questions answered by Mr Tsahkna and Ms Tuus-Laul. Now to the amendments. Amendment 1 amends the title of the draft. The change in the title of the draft act was due to the need to bring the title into line with the norm rule. The title of the draft in question reflects the amendment of three laws, the State Family Benefits Act, the Social Welfare Act and the Act on granting international protection to an alien. At the same time, the subject matter of the draft is the amendment of four laws. There is also an amendment to the State Family Benefits Act, the Social Welfare Act and the Labour Market Services and Benefits Act. If the draft law amending or repealing the law proposes to amend or repeal more than three laws, the title of the law to be amended shall state the title of the law whose amendment is due to the principal purpose of the draft law. The title of the bill must show that the law of amendment is proposed and that more than one law is being amended. Thus, during the second reading, the title was "Proposal to amend the National Family Benefits Act and other laws'. Amendment 2 was tabled by the Group of the Fatherland and Res Publica Alliance. According to the author of the amendment, the amendment is motivated by an increase in the risk of poverty as the number of children increases, and from the third child onwards it will increase considerably. In order to combat child poverty, it is particularly important to support families at the highest risk of poverty with universal support, i.e. families of many children with three or more children. It had also been said that the increase in child benefits for third and more children must not be limited to EUR 4.1, but must be at least as high as for the first and second children. The initiator of the draft act did not support this amendment. The initiator's representative pointed out that the child benefit for the third child and for each subsequent child is currently EUR 76,72. As of 1 January 2015, this contribution will increase to EUR 100 per month according to the draft. The amount of this allowance is therefore increased by EUR 23,28 per child per month and not by EUR 4,1. According to the amendment adopted in 2012, the child benefit for the third child and for each subsequent child is paid at a rate of 10 times the child benefit, i.e. EUR 95,90 per child per month. The date of entry into force of this amendment is 1 January 2015. The amount of child benefit shall be EUR 9,59. Since this bill has been adopted, but the version of the law is not valid, i.e. the law has not entered into force, it cannot be argued that child benefit for the third child and for each subsequent child is only increased by EUR 4.1 per month. Thus, the child benefit for the third and each subsequent child of the family will increase over time, which will increase by EUR 23.28 per child per month in the same order as the child benefit for the first and second child of the family, which will increase to EUR 45 per month, i.e. an increase of EUR 25.28. The third and fourth amendments have been tabled by the Group of the Centre Party. The amendment was clarified by the need to establish a correlation between child support and general economic development. The indexation of child benefit provides for an adjustment of the annual amount of child benefit in line with the increase in the consumer price index and the income from social tax. The index is calculated on the basis of an 80% change in the income of the previous year's social tax and a 20% change in the consumer price index of the previous year. According to the authors of the amendment, the aim of the amendment is to ensure that child benefit is adjusted to reflect annual inflation. As the amendments to the State Family Benefits Act will enter into force on 1 January 2015, the indexation of child benefit under the amendment will take effect from the beginning of 2016. The initiator of the draft act does not support the proposal of the Centre Party Group, as the above amendment is based on § 26 of the State Pension Insurance Act, which provides for the indexation of the state pension. The full implementation side has been neglected. According to the initiator of the draft act, it is not possible to implement the proposal in this form. According to the State Pension Insurance Act, the pension index is established by a regulation of the Government of the Republic. The Government of the Republic shall approve the index by 20 March of the current year at the latest and the index shall be used from 1 April. The amendment of the Centre Party Group does not provide for a power of attorney to confirm the index. The initiator of the draft act also said that the pension index could not be applied to the indexation of family benefits. The social tax is 33% divided into two parts: 13% health insurance and 20% pension insurance. Only 20% of the pension insurance component is used to find the pension index. However, in order to index family benefits, it is desirable to use the entire social tax. The representatives of the initiator of the draft act also said that no analysis of the impact of the indexation of grants has been carried out, nor did they take into account how IT solutions are being implemented. They said that the development of the National Pension Insurance Register had to be carried out. At present, it is not possible to index family allowances because there is no programme and the change is very large, labour-intensive and expensive. The members of the Social Affairs Committee agreed to the reasons given by the representative of the Ministry of Social Affairs and did not support the amendments tabled by the IRL Group and the Central Party Group. Result of the vote: 3 votes for, 5 against and no abstentions. Now the procedural decisions of the lead committee. At its meeting on 3 June, the Committee on Social Affairs decided to put the draft at second reading on the agenda for the plenary session on 11 June and proposed that the second reading be closed. At its meeting on 9 June, the Committee on Social Affairs approved the second-reading procedural documents and decided that, if the second reading of the draft was completed, the third reading of the draft would take place on Wednesday of the following week, i.e. 18 June. Heljo Pikhof was elected rapporteur for the commission. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-11_U907	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Ladies and gentlemen, Despite the fact that quite a lot has been said about the numbers and content of this draft, I am still happy to speak up on this very important and good draft once again on the basis of my own views. The Reform Party has always considered that one child living in need is too much for Estonia. As a result, a reform of the need-based increase in child support was launched two years ago in order to support children in deprived families more effectively than before. Today we have reached the second reading of the draft law amending the State Family Benefits Act. I will repeat here the exact amounts and figures. As agreed by the Government Union of the Estonian Reform Party and the Social Democratic Party on 20 March 2014, this draft law intends to increase child support for the first and second children to 45 euros per month and for the third and each subsequent child to 100 euros per month. According to the Coalition Agreement, the need-based family allowance paid to families living in relative poverty also doubles that paid to other families. When determining income support, the proportion of the child is increased by equalising that of the adult family member. The aim is to support families with children more and more effectively. All universal child benefits will increase from the first child of the family. Child benefits are set as a specific amount and no longer depend on the amount of child benefits. This is a monthly allowance paid from the birth of the child until the age of 16. If the child continues his/her studies, the child benefit will continue until the child reaches the age of 19. Thus, the basis for calculating family benefits, including child benefits, remains the same as the general principles currently in force. You can apply for child benefit retroactively within six months of your entitlement. As we become the law of the bill, we will take a step closer to the goal of reducing child poverty and improving the livelihood of families. The law therefore makes children's consumption of income support equal to that of the first member of the family, which means that the income support for families with children will increase. The need-based family allowance paid to families with low incomes will also be increased. The adoption of the draft is accompanied by certain changes to the state budget. The amount of money paid for child benefits in the state budget will increase by around 73 million euros in 2015, which means that in 2015 the total cost of child benefit will be more than 151.4 million euros. That's a lot of money. Indeed, those in need must necessarily be supported. But let's ask, are all beneficiaries in need and inevitably need of this money? Some time ago, I proposed giving families the opportunity to give up a part or even a whole child benefit if they feel that they do not need it. There are certainly families in Estonia whose budget is neither the current child allowance of EUR 19 nor the increase to EUR 45, because the financial situation of the family is good enough. What if these families were offered the opportunity to give up all or part of the child benefit? Are we ready for this? We could, for example, set up a public foundation to support vulnerable families in which to place this money. I think it would be reasonable to move towards a more responsible society where public support is not taken for granted, but when it is received, the question is whether no one else needs this money more. This attitude, in turn, will help to keep taxes low and promote an economic environment with fewer redistribution, which in turn will ensure a better life for all. I'd like three more minutes.
ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-11_U908	Please, three more minutes!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-11_U909	By proposing to those who feel that, thanks to their incomes, they no longer need state support for children, I sincerely hope that there are many people in our society who would like to join this idea. There is currently no initiative group or working group. There is only a thought, but it can also be the beginning of an act. However, obviously this idea requires getting used to and discussion. How the system should work is hard to say in one stroke. In our e-country, it is possible that, for example, when completing the tax return, the grant for the coming year will be waived by marking the line for that purpose. We are used to thinking and saying that children are the top priority in our society and without them our country has no meaning or future. ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-11_U911	Ladies and gentlemen, The Isamaa and Res Publica Association welcomes the fact that child benefits are increasing, that children's share in coping is growing to the full, and that attention has finally been paid to child poverty in families where one or two children are growing, in addition to what has already been done in the initiative of the Isamaa and Res Publica Association and the Reform Party. There is, however, one fundamental thing that came out of this discussion and that makes us deeply concerned about all the good things in the bill. When we look at the in-depth studies that have been carried out in Estonia on poverty, we see that families with three or more children and single parents are at the greatest risk of poverty in all households. Childrenless families with two working adults live best in the Republic of Estonia. The difference between these two ends is enormous. It is precisely because childhood is actually a risk of poverty that has been analysed at length and in depth in the country's current policies, supported by ministries and Statistics Estonia's data, that it is understood that, yes, every child must be supported, but, above all, families with many children must be supported. Although the proportion of families with many children is extremely low, families with many children make up only 2.5% of all households, one in five Estonian children is growing there. The Fatherland and Res Publica Association are not in a position to adopt an approach in which families with many children must go out asking for alms. Yes, there is a growing need for child support, but we consider it deeply abnormal that public policies that have made progress so far are being cut down and since the third child there is no longer a large, perceived increase in child support, but that this support is only increasing by EUR 4.1 compared to the law that we have adopted today. I'm actually embarrassed about the bathroom. It is not difficult to say that this law was passed in 2012, and according to the law currently in force on 1 January 2015, all the longer-term accounts of the state budget have already been made for this purpose, and from the third child child child child allowance will indeed increase to EUR 95,9. So, in essence, we will only add EUR 4.1 with this draft. When I take the speech of the Chairman of the Social Democratic Party Group on 21 May, in the seven minutes he was able to speak, there were seven mistakes in the speech. I'm gonna use this arena here to fix these mistakes. The first false claim was that child benefits are now increasing for the first time in over 10 years. That statement is a lie! Child benefits have increased to 76.72 euros per child since the third child, according to the Act adopted in 2012, and have increased to 95.9 euros per child since 1 January 2015. The needs-based child benefit for the first and second children was also introduced as early as 2013. In 2013, we also implemented the need-based education allowance for free higher education, in 2008 home support for families with many children, and in that time the 1500 families who needed it most have received it. Since 2006, the second child's income tax exemption has been applied. 2013 became an addition to the parental pension, the foundation of which has now been considerably shaken. So there is no way to agree that in 10 years nothing has been done to increase child benefits. Nor is it true that on 1 January 2015, the third and each subsequent child's allowance will increase from EUR 76,72 to EUR 100 per month. What is true is that the grant will increase by EUR 4.1, i.e. EUR 100 per month, although the nominal amount as stated in the explanatory memorandum to the amendment proposed by the Fatherland and Res Publica Union should have increased to EUR 120. This EUR 20 has unfortunately been a loss for the coalition to give to those families where poverty is greatest and where the need for this money is greatest. The third child's allowance will not increase EUR 5 but EUR 4, the difference of only 20%, but it is a good thing that this has been paid attention to in previous speeches. More than twofold increase in universal child support is indeed an important step towards increasing the sense of security in families with one or two children. In families where more children are growing, i.e. one in five children in Estonia, we cannot talk about increasing the sense of security in a situation where any expectation of an increase in child benefits already adopted by law is considered unreasonable. Tricking with child benefits certainly does not increase the sense of family security. And we hope that in the future, there will be so much respect for our smallest companions in Estonian society in this Chamber that we will not try to show ourselves larger by lying about the decisions that have already been taken. When we come back to the next mistake, planning of children's costs in the longer term has been highlighted. As we read in the explanatory memorandum to the draft regarding the rejection of the proposal of the Fatherland and Res Publica Association, any long-term planning is stamped unreasonable. This, of course, is not what we sign. I sum up the most important thing: children do not have to mean poverty, and a family with a child does not have to mean that you have to go and ask for help. We consider it extremely important that child support also grows proportionally to third and more children in the family to at least EUR 120 per month as from 1 January 2015. We ask for this amendment to be supported in the full chamber of the Riigikogu. Thank you for your attention.
